A 2019 joint report published by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW) and [Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_Bloomberg_School_of_Public_Health) stated that most sanctions were focused on abusive officials involved in corruption, did not target the economy,[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-338][^cite_note-HRW2019Report-309] and that the 2017 sanctions allowed exceptions for food and medicine.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-309] Consulting firm [ANOVA Policy Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANOVA_Policy_Research?action=edit\&redlink=1) stated in 2021 that the sanctions were linked to a decrease in monthly oil production, and increases in monthly food and medicine imports; it found no evidence of negative effects on food and medicine imports, but wrote that the economic data did not account for price controls on imported products being abandoned in 2017.[^cite_note-ANOVA-339] In 2018, [Susana Raffalli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susana_Raffalli) had stated that 36% of Venezuelan children had [stunted growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stunted_growth) prior to sanctions; she cited the [PDVAL affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDVAL_affair) (tons of imported food supplies found rotten during [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez)'s government) as an example of food shortages before sanctions.[^cite_note-340]

*[The Lancet](463899.md)* journal editors noted in 2019 that Maduro had used food as a political weapon and resisted humanitarian aid, and that the U.S. had reacted with sanctions that they said resulted in collateral food and medicine shortages.[^cite_note-Lancet-316] The editors called for the involvement of non-governmental entities to provide distribution of food and medicine, and for the Venezuelan government to allow them to do so, and stated that the UN Human Rights Council considers economic sanctions a violation of human rights.[^cite_note-Lancet-316]

An April 2019 report by [Mark Weisbrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Weisbrot) and [Jeffrey Sachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Sachs) claimed that a 31% rise in deaths between 2017 and 2018 was due to the 2017 sanctions, and that 40,000 people in Venezuela may have died as a result;[^cite_note-Independent-319][^cite_note-Agence_Press-France-341] Weisbrot stated that he "could not prove those excess deaths were the result of sanctions, but said the increase ran parallel to the imposition of the measures and an attendant fall in oil production".[^cite_note-Independent-319] *[The Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Independent)* wrote that similar concerns had been raised earlier by UN representatives.[^cite_note-Independent-319] The report's findings and methodology were described as invalid and disputed by other economists,[^cite_note-DontBlame-342] *[La Patilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Patilla)*,[^cite_note-343] the US State department,[^cite_note-Independent-319] and the Brookings Institution, who stated that most of the decline pre-dated the sanctions and that the methodology was flawed or speculative.[^cite_note-344] [Opposition-aligned](75340539.md) academic and Guaido appointee [Ricardo Hausmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Hausmann)[^cite_note-345] and Frank Muci published a rebuttal in *[Americas Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas_Quarterly)*, stating that the analysis took Colombia as a [counterfactual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterfactual_conditional) for Venezuela, when Colombia and Venezuela are "radically different in other dimensions".[^cite_note-DontBlame-342] They argued that oil production trends between the two nations were different in the decade before sanctions and that a month after the 2017 sanctions, Maduro replaced the PDVSA president with an inexperienced military general who restructured the oil entity, worsening its performance.[^cite_note-DontBlame-342]

### Public perception

See also: [Polls in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polls_in_Venezuela)

Polling in 2023 by Datanalisis found that 74% of Venezuelans do not support sanctions, 30% attribute Venezuela's problems to the sanctions, and half of Venezuelans agree with the sanctions on some administration officials.[^cite_note-ElPais2023-303] The director of Datanalisis stated that most Venezuelans recognize the government's blame for the sanctions, but have moved away from supporting them because their objectives have not been achieved and have worsened the lives of citizens.[^cite_note-ElPais2023-303] A poll by DatinCorp conducted among Venezuelans in 2019 found that 68% believed that the sanctions have affected their quality of life.[^cite_note-CSIS-290][^cite_note-346]

En términos nutricionales, quizás tenemos el mejor indicador de que esta crisis no data del último año de sanciones o de medidas externas. Cuando una nación da cuenta de un 36% de niños con un retardo en crecimiento, se da cuenta biológica de un rezago nutricional, afectivo y social de al menos siete años. De eso dan cuenta también los índices de escasez de alimentos. Para mencionar un contrapeso: (...) las toneladas de carne y de cerdo perdidas, tenemos todavía la impunidad, señor representante del Estado, el caso de PDVAL, con toneladas de comida descompuesta, importadas por el Estado, que no fueron distribuidas, lo que nos hace pensar que fueron importadas con fines no alimentarios, sino posiblemente con fines de acceder a dólares preferenciales.
